Abstract: Coding agents are autonomous developers. Given a request, they change the code and report back when done. As such, they can handle not only independent requests but whole chains of them, each request built on the last. Agents are known to report wrong work as done, yet benchmarks still test them one request at a time, on human-written code, checked once. We build BOBA-Bench, 280 chains of 20 consecutive requests drawn from 140 real open-source projects, and evaluate ten coding agents on both. All ten do far worse on chains. On average, they complete 95% of requests taken one at a time, but when requests are chained together, they complete 51%. Worse code is not the cause. Once checked, agent code is nearly as good a base for later requests as human code. The cause is the mistakes agents miss. Coding agents declare a request done once their own tests pass, even when those tests miss a mistake. The next request then builds on broken code and fails about twice as often. The mistake is rarely found later. Nor can agents catch such mistakes by reviewing code. Asked whether an implementation is correct, they are right 53% of the time, barely better than a coin flip. Coding agents are therefore heavily dependent on access to verification. A single external ``that's wrong'' after a failed request and one repair round lift chain completion from 51% to 59%, and a repaired mistake no longer hurts the requests that follow. In simpler terms, coding agents are only as reliable as the checks they can reach: without verification, every request that follows is built on broken assumptions.

</details>

Abstract: AI coding agents rely on repository-level instruction files to understand project conventions, development workflows, and constraints. Abstract: Root cause analysis (RCA) in microservice systems requires distinguishing failure sources from routine fluctuations and propagated symptoms. Small language models (SLMs) offer a practical solution under cost and privacy constraints, and recent approaches improve their capabilities through structured diagnostic procedures. However, performing diagnostic analysis does not ensure correct root-cause identification. Our empirical study shows that final decisions often favor large anomaly scores despite the model's own propagation analysis suggesting otherwise. Moreover, the input does not reveal whether these apparently anomalous signals are common during normal operation. These findings motivate our system, which improves how SLMs assess evidence and select root causes through Historical Frequency Profiling and Biased-Decision Correction. Historical Frequency Profiling quantifies how often metric and log signals are flagged as anomalous in the fault-free period, providing context for distinguishing routine deviations from potential failure evidence. Biased-Decision Correction identifies points where the model reaches different conclusions from the same initial analysis and uses preference optimization to favor more accurate diagnoses. Against baselines including the 14B OpsAgent and RCA-Agent with commercial models, our system with a 4B model achieves the highest or tied-highest accuracy on all four datasets, reaching 48.7% on Bank compared with 18.5% for OpsAgent.

Abstract: Development teams need reproducible tasks from their own repositories and workflows to evaluate coding agents. Conversational developer-agent collaboration ("vibe coding") produces abundant coding sessions and repository checkpoints, but requirements evolve across turns, unrelated conversations co-occur with commits, and engineering efforts can span multiple sessions. We present SessionFarmer, an automated framework that turns these records into self-contained, executable software engineering tasks. It analyzes dialogue and commit history to filter unrelated sessions and group multi-turn, potentially multi-session interactions into work episodes centered on one engineering goal. For each episode, it reconstructs a hermetic execution container, synthesizes a task statement from the full context, and authors repository-grounded engineering-quality rubrics and unit tests. Closed-loop test refinement uses requirement-breaking mutants and a blind solver to reduce false rejections of valid alternatives and false acceptances of incomplete solutions. We construct CropChat, an open benchmark of 242 tasks across 61 public repositories and 14 programming languages, and CropEnterprise from proprietary enterprise logs. In a CropEnterprise audit, the original developers endorse the vast majority of reconstructed episode boundaries, task statements, and quality rubrics. Full-episode context covers substantially more user requirements than single-turn or single-session inputs. Test refinement cuts false-rejection rates and raises balanced accuracy against reference patch labels by 7-9 percentage points across solvers. Across 12 model-harness configurations on CropChat, the strongest setup achieves 39.3% Pass@1, while harness choice shifts Pass@1 by up to 8.4 percentage points for the same model.

Abstract: LLM agents call tools that delete data or move money through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). The specification asks for a human who can deny tool calls but splits the mechanism between the server, which can annotate a tool or ask the user through the host (elicitation), and the host application, which decides whether to prompt. We measure both sides from source code. In a 1,000-repository sample of the official MCP registry, an estimated 90% of destructive tools (95% CI 82-95) have no server-side confirmation, and most servers that elicit proceed, or fall back to a flag the model can set, when the host cannot relay the question. Of 41 popular open-source hosts and agent SDKs, 32 have no approval step by default, including 16 of 25 applications. Pairing sampled destructive tools flagged by a rule-based extractor with these applications, 60.7% of pairs have no enforced human approval by default. Through four agent SDKs, four of five low-cost models executed an injected deletion in 479 of 960 runs. If hosts prompted for every tool not annotated read-only or non-destructive, the unapproved share of these pairs would fall to 5.4%; universal elicitation support would leave 56.7%. Applications should therefore configure approval.

Abstract: The rapid growth of automated code generation has created a pressing need for automated code review. Yet code review resists full automation: it depends on context beyond the \textit{diff}, on the author's intent, and on judgments about design and maintainability on which even expert reviewers disagree. Existing code review benchmarks lack standardization and rely largely on noisy human review comments scored with text-similarity metrics, which reward surface overlap and fail to measure precision. We introduce \texttt{CR-Bench}, a defect-centric evaluation framework that recasts SWE-Bench issues as pull-request review tasks with full repository context. After filtering for review-detectable defects and grounding each to a single originating pull request, \texttt{CR-Bench} contains 584 instances, with a 174-instance \texttt{CR-Bench-Verified} subset derived from SWE-Bench Verified. Each instance pairs a pull request with a known defect, its ground-truth fix, and a taxonomy label for bug category, impact, and severity. We propose two complementary metrics: \emph{Known-Defect Detection Rate} (KDDR), which measures whether an agent identifies the labeled defect, and \emph{Usefulness Rate}, which measures the fraction of review comments that are actionable. We evaluate eight code review agents over four backbones, plus commercially available Gemini Code Assist, using three independent LLM judges. Even the strongest systems detect only about one-third of known defects (KDDR $\approx$ 33\%), and Gemini Code Assist detects 8.1\%.

Abstract: Invalid issue reports are prevalent in open source software (OSS) projects, imposing substantial burdens on maintainers who already face a high risk of burnout. Prior research has primarily focused on identifying invalid reports for specific issue categories (e.g., bug and wontfix), leaving the broader landscape of OSS issue types largely unexplored and limiting the generalizability of existing approaches. Moreover, existing methods provide little support for explaining or validating their predictions, reducing their practical usefulness for maintainers. With the rise of LLM-based agents, it remains unclear whether a comprehensive understanding of the causes of invalidity across all issue types can improve validity assessment performance while producing well-grounded rationales. To this end, we first manually annotated the validity status of 520 issue reports from three OSS projects, i.e., pandas, scikit-learn, and mypy, resulting in 257 invalid reports and 263 valid ones. Then, we conducted a thematic analysis on these invalid reports and identified 13 causes of invalidity that are closely specific to issue types. Based on these insights, we propose IssueLighter, an agent-based framework with a type-aware validity assessment strategy that dynamically executes commands. Experimental results show that IssueLighter consistently and substantially outperforms all baseline methods, with an average F1 improvement of over 20 percentage points. Ablation studies confirm that both the type-aware strategy and the taxonomy of causes of invalidity contribute to the performance gains. Qualitative analysis further demonstrates that IssueLighter generates rationales grounded in accurately identified causes of invalidity and supported by evidence. Moreover, leveraging these rationales, we challenged maintainers' original decisions in four disputed cases, and all received approval.

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are now embedded in enterprise workflows, yet their outputs remain prone to hallucinations: plausible-sounding but factually incorrect or malformed content. Most verification approaches are reactive, detecting errors only after generation and often spending additional expensive LLM calls to do so. We present COVE, a task-adaptive, code-based ensemble that takes the opposite stance: it prevents errors before generation and validates structure and consistency afterwards using six deterministic, pure-Python innovations that add no extra LLM calls. On an internal evaluation spanning 11 text datasets (1,020 examples) and 4 image datasets (200 examples), COVE lifts text accuracy from a 45.2% unverified baseline to 82.1% (an 81.7% relative improvement), improves image accuracy by 69.5%, and reduces token usage by 48 to 96% (simulated cost model), at a measured verification overhead of 0.26 ms per output (excluding the LLM call). This article describes the engineering design of each innovation (with pseudocode and the exact parameters we use), the measured impact, an ablation of every component, and the practical lessons learned from deploying code-based verification in enterprise pipelines.

Abstract: Repository-level AI coding benchmarks measure task success but do not directly capture the conditions required for software maintenance completion. Using a fixed 18-criterion taxonomy, we analyze 2,925 Apache Fineract issues and 944 tasks from SWE-bench Pro, SWE-PolyBench Java, and SWE-EVO.   Among 1,177 benchmark task pairs matched on issue classification, modified-file count, and code churn, the mean fulfillment-set Jaccard distance is 0.8138, and 57.4% have disjoint condition sets. Condition count alone does not explain this contrast: SWE-bench Pro averages 2.47 conditions per task, versus 1.78 for Fineract. Temporal (B4) and numerical (B2) conditions have a phi coefficient of +0.430 in Fineract versus -0.013 in the pooled benchmark corpus. Among temporally tagged tasks, numerical conditions are also specified in 48.85% (254/520) of Fineract cases versus 5.81% (5/86) of benchmark cases.
  We document 16 directional relationships in Fineract requirements involving time, calculation, and state preservation, and inspect four benchmark cases to illustrate how relevant conditions appear in evaluation assertions. These examples do not quantify benchmark-wide oracle omissions. The results show that similar surface task profiles can mask substantially different fulfillment-condition sets. We recommend reporting condition breadth, co-occurrence, and directional relationships separately. The corpus comparisons are descriptive and may reflect domain differences; they do not establish causality or production failure rates.

Abstract: Repairing obsolete tests in response to source code evolution is critical for test suite reliability and software quality assurance. While large language models (LLMs) have emerged as promising tools for automating many software development tasks, the effective supply of context and the orchestration of repair pipelines in this source--test co-evolution scenario remain poorly understood. Moreover, although reasoning is found helpful for LLMs on some complex coding tasks, it remains unclear which reasoning steps actually contribute to effective test repairs and how the insights learned from LLMs' behaviors benefit coding agents. In this paper, we present a systematic empirical study of test repair for source-test co-evolution empowered by LLMs, investigating the effective setups and typical LLM behaviors. To support this study, we construct a new benchmark of 500 real-world source-test co-evolution cases from commits submitted between May 2024 and April 2026 across 15 highly-starred open-source Java projects. Our experimental results reveal several key insights for effective LLM-based test repair: the complementary benefits of combining test failure symptoms, source code changes, and supportive code context, as well as the effectiveness of self-repair rounds. We further derive a common reasoning action taxonomy from LLMs' reasoning traces, which proves instrumental in guiding LLMs to repair tests more effectively while at a lower financial cost. Applying our findings to the Codex agent further improves its repair success rate on challenging cases by 66.7%.

Abstract: Recently, harness self-evolution has emerged as an important paradigm for improving agent performance. Prior studies show that evolving the agent harness can effectively enhance agent capabilities without modifying the foundation model's parameters. We find that existing approaches largely study agent evolution on a single task. In practice, coding agents may need to handle multiple task types over time. Whether an agent harness can continuously evolve across multiple tasks without suffering from catastrophic forgetting remains an important open question.
  In this paper, we investigate this open question through a comprehensive empirical study of continual harness self-evolution. We organize three software engineering task datasets into six different evolution directions and apply two representative harness evolution methods to perform continual evolution along these directions. Our experimental results show that evolution on a subsequent dataset can substantially undermine the performance on previously seen datasets. For example, with DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731, Meta-Harness improves its performance on SWE-Bench to 94.0% after evolution. However, after further evolution on FeatureBench, its performance on SWE-Bench drops to 32.0% by the fifth round, corresponding to a 66% relative decrease. Finally, based on our experimental findings, we identify several insights, providing directions for the continual self-evolution of agent harnesses. Code is available at https://github.com/THU-Agent/continual-harness-evolution.

Abstract: Agentic AI is shifting developers' responsibilities from software implementation to software governance. While this paradigm improves productivity, it also raises challenges in ensuring that autonomous coding agents preserve developers' intentions. Among these, operationalizing fairness-by-design is largely unexplored, as existing approaches provide limited support for representing and propagating fairness requirements throughout agentic development workflows. In this paper, we investigate whether specification-driven development can operationalize fairness-by-design in the agentic development of machine learning systems. We study three coding agents on four machine learning applications, using four prompts that progressively operationalize fairness through nine Fairness Specification Elements, and analyze the traceability of fairness requirements across the generated artifacts, the fairness-accuracy trade-off of the resulting models, and the agents' design decisions and rationales. The results show that when fairness is operationalized, agents capture almost all fairness requirements in the specification (97.22%) and preserve most of them in the code (82.41%). However, the resulting models are not significantly fairer, as agents implement fairness as a post-hoc audit that neither shapes training nor prevents model delivery. Specifications thus make fairness traceable, but not enforceable, calling for fairness specifications that state what agents should do when requirements are not met, and for workflows that make the fairness decisions agents delegate to developers explicit.

</details>

Abstract: Existing large language model (LLM) backdoor attacks operate primarily in chat settings, where the risk is bounded by free-form text outputs. AI agents remove that bound by performing actions and executing commands on the user's machine. We introduce structured-output backdoor attacks against reasoning models in agentic coding settings. A trigger placed anywhere in the agent's context causes the model to emit an attacker-chosen tool call, which the harness executes, directly compromising the system's confidentiality, integrity, or availability. We present Kerkoporta, a methodology that constructs high-quality poison samples, injects the backdoor via supervised fine-tuning, and recovers utility loss with lightweight reinforcement learning. We evaluate on modern, heavily post-trained open-weight models across proprietary and open-source harnesses, focusing on coding for its wide adoption and long-horizon task structure. The attack reaches success rates of up to 96.2% and can be configured to generalize across harnesses or bind to a single target. The resulting models can outperform the clean baseline on standard utility benchmarks, giving victims a direct incentive to adopt them. Overall, we demonstrate that LLM backdoors can have critical security impacts in the context of modern coding agents, and we characterize the poisoning methodology required to insert backdoors that are both effective and stealthy in this setting.

</details>

Abstract: Defense systems for maritime assets must distinguish approaching benign craft from uncrewed surface vessels (USVs) with sufficient warning to act. Shore cameras using computer vision can separate hostile vessels from benign traffic, but these systems may be attacked using an adversarial hull patch or physical disguise. We evaluate adversarial hull patches and find they suppress detection in up to 29% of attempts but never cause misclassification as benign. Physical disguise, however, can bypass both a camera check and a radar presence check. To evaluate defenses, we develop a hybrid replay protocol from automatic identification system (AIS) tracks that combines recorded benign motion with direct or off-axis attack run-ins. We find that for recreational-labeled vessels attacking via direct run-in within 4 nautical miles, an asset-relative closing-rate check flags 94-95% at 15-20 knots, compared with 12% for a track-only speed check. The checks are complementary on off-axis approaches, constraining the attacker to remain below both thresholds and extending the time required to reach the asset. Flagging on either check has a 4.9% held-out false-alarm rate overall and 12.3% on recreational traffic. The replay-based evaluation protocol supports comparative test and evaluation of defense methods.

Abstract: We present practical preimage attacks on 19- and 20-step SHA-256 that respect the standard IV, Davies-Meyer feed-forward, and full FIPS 180-4 padding, for arbitrary prescribed 256-bit targets. Building on Basra's algebraic selector saturation and backward padding absorption, we introduce three techniques: an exact $O(1)$ inversion of the low padding byte $(W_{13} \bmod 256) = \mathtt{0x80}$ that leaves $(a_5, a_6)$ free for exact context shaping; a causal bit-slice equivalence theorem that prunes $99.74\%$ of right-hand candidate nodes across seven stages; and a closed-form $\mathbf{F}_2$ upper-half inverse paired with compact $Q_0^{-1}$ lookup tables. One CPU core solves arbitrary 19-step padded targets in $1.2$--$86.4$ s (median $19.5$ s) after an $18$ s table build. A 20-step padded preimage costs $2^{46.6}$ CPU-reference evaluations: $191$ node-hours on one 10-core Apple M4 CPU node (16 threads), or $9.0$--$22.2$ GPU-hours on one NVIDIA L4. A companion Zero-$a_3$ bridge inverts the unpadded 20-step compression function in $2^{38.1}$ evaluations. We provide 46 self-verifying witnesses, including 55-byte preimages of SHA-256("abc") and $0^{256}$. These attacks cover 19--20 of 64 steps and do not threaten full SHA-256.

</details>

Abstract: Economic-state manipulation has emerged as a significant security threat to decentralized finance (DeFi), causing substantial financial losses. Existing studies predominantly characterize these attacks as price manipulation, focusing on observable price distortions while overlooking underlying violations of economic-state integrity. We conduct a comprehensive empirical study of Economic-State Manipulation (ESM) vulnerabilities in DeFi. We first curate a diverse dataset comprising 276 real-world attacks and 91 Code4rena audit findings. Based on these cases, we develop a cause-oriented taxonomy comprising four primary categories and eleven subcategories. Our analysis reveals that ESM vulnerabilities extend beyond conventional price manipulation, frequently involving violations of protocol-internal accounting, AMM state consistency, and execution-time constraints. We further investigate the evaluation datasets of six existing studies and find that, although they cover 6-10 vulnerability subcategories, their distributions remain dominated by limited manipulation mechanisms. Abstract: Existing studies of vulnerable pre-installed Android apps identify dangerous behavior by checking whether an app invokes an API from a predefined set of security-sensitive APIs. These sets usually exclude generic APIs to avoid false positives; unfortunately, this misses a class of vulnerabilities in which a generic API, such as a database read, appears benign in general but becomes security-sensitive when operating on protected data, yet is left without sufficient access control. While this class of vulnerabilities appears addressable via access control analysis, existing studies focus only on the presence of checks, not on whether those checks enforce sufficient protection for the operation at a given callsite. In this work, we propose Callix, which first assesses callsite sensitivity by jointly analyzing the invoked API functionality and the data involved in the operation, then recovers each security check on the execution path as an explicit security property, and finally introduces the notion of restriction context to decide whether these checks are sufficient for the identified sensitive operation. We evaluated Callix on 1,750 distinct pre-installed apps from the 10 top-tier Android vendors as of 2025. In total, Callix discovered 819 vulnerable paths across these vendors, 42.1% of which allow reading or modifying private user data through generic database operations. Manual validation of a random 60% sample shows a precision of 95.1%. Many of our findings have been confirmed and rewarded through vendor bug bounty programs.

Abstract: Language-model agents commonly collapse two decisions that security systems separate: selecting an action and determining whether that action is authorized. When a model exercises standing authority, indirect prompt injection can convert influence over its reasoning into unauthorized effects. We present AgentCap, a runtime authorization layer that externalizes this decision from the model and mediates every tool invocation through an MCP wrapper. Each request declares an intent, and AgentCap evaluates policy constraints over trust, workflow prerequisites, mutually exclusive authority, and the provenance of security-sensitive arguments. Authorized requests receive short-lived, budget-bounded capability tokens that are validated at the tool boundary. Delegation is restricted to session-bound parent-child chains that preserve action and resource while budget and lifetime can only be attenuated, preventing authority from being expanded or reused across unrelated agent sessions. A declarative policy language provides a common representation for runtime enforcement and predeployment analysis. Across AgentDojo and InjecAgent, AgentCap reduces attack success in every evaluated model-benchmark setting, including zero attack success with GPT-5.4 on both benchmarks. Controlled adversarial scenarios further show that AgentCap blocks every attempted unauthorized execution (ASR 0%) while preserving all legitimate operations exercised. These results demonstrate that agent security requires authorization of model-proposed effects at the tool boundary, independently of the model's reasoning.

</details>

Abstract: The communication topology of Large Language Model Multi-Agent Systems (LLM-MASs) governs how information propagates among agents and encodes critical domain knowledge and expert workflow design. It is valuable intellectual property and plays a decisive role in system utility and safety, yet remains vulnerable to inference attacks that stealthily reconstruct it from agent outputs. Existing defenses detect or mitigate such attacks but do not proactively conceal the topology itself. We propose \textit{Illusion}, a proactive topology obfuscation defense for LLM-MAS. \textit{Illusion} injects fake edges and fake messages to present attackers with a deceptive topology, while selecting low-impact candidates that preserve output-distribution equivalence and task-accuracy non-inferiority. The resulting fake edges are designed to be indistinguishable from genuine messages under black-box observation. Extensive experiments show that \textit{Illusion} reduces edge-level F1 from over 0.87 to 0.62 and graph similarity from over 0.94 to 0.71. It also largely preserves output distributions and task performance, and its fake edges resist both similarity-based and LLM-judge filtering, even under adaptive pruning.

Abstract: Reliable first-seen dates are important for constructing temporal malware evaluation splits, but this information often comes from external services that are not equally accessible to all researchers. We investigate whether machine learning can instead estimate an executable's first-seen date or determine when its PE/COFF compilation timestamp is unreliable. Using EMBER2024, we evaluate feature-based and raw-byte models across classification, regression, ranking, and latent-reliability formulations. Direct first-seen regression produces errors comparable to a substantial portion of the dataset's collection window, and none of the evaluated scores provides a useful chronological ordering of files. Current models therefore cannot replace externally sourced first-seen metadata. In contrast, classifiers can identify timestamps that fall within specified tolerances of first seen and can detect clearly implausible timestamps with high discrimination. We also find substantial timestamp disagreement among both benign and malicious executables, indicating that unreliable timestamps are not exclusively a product of malicious manipulation. These results establish baselines and identify timestamp reliability as a distinct direction for future security-ML research.

Abstract: Anonymous credentials can hide which registered client participates in federated learning, but their unlinkability also makes repeated misconduct difficult to associate. We present VeilFL, a two-aggregator protocol that adds staged accountability to PINE (USENIX Security '24). Each submission binds two private PINE messages, a round-specific pseudonym, and a tracing pair to the same hidden credential secret. This design enables detection of duplicate submissions within a round without introducing a persistent identifier for normal participation. VeilFL implements accountability in two stages. After a confirmed duplicate or joint PINE rejection, a threshold tracing committee reveals a stable handle that links faults associated with the same credential. The client's registered identity is recoverable only after the public log records the requisite number of linked faults and a separate threshold committee authorizes identification. Accepted and unopened submissions remain unlinkable under the stated assumptions. This work establishes submission binding and duplicate detection, anonymity, fault non-frameability, controlled two-stage opening, and preservation of PINE's privacy and validity guarantees. In the prototype implementation, creating a submission requires a median of 2.11 ms, while verification requires 3.01 ms. Batch verification reduces the cost to 1.86 ms per submission for a batch of 1,000. The public part of each submission is 608 bytes, with 1,248 bytes of additional VeilFL protocol data across the two aggregator submissions. Each opening stage completes within 4.3 ms for the different committee configurations.

</details>

Abstract: Decentralizing large language model (LLM) inference across untrusted Web nodes requires robust verification to ensure computational integrity. Existing activation-based verification and detection mechanisms validate intermediate outputs against reference values, permitting bounded numerical deviations to accommodate benign cross-hardware discrepancies. However, a critical security question remains: what can a malicious worker achieve while operating within these tolerance bounds?
  In this work, we introduce Hush-Up, a stealthy availability attack against distributed LLM inference, where a single malicious worker halts generation while evading attribution. The attacker first profiles its local execution to model an empirical bound of benign hardware errors. Using this error bound as a feasibility constraint, the attacker optimizes an adversarial perturbation on its output activations to maximize the final-layer probability of the end-of-sequence (EOS) token, i.e., to hush up the model. Across diverse models and benchmarks, Hush-Up effectively forces premature generation termination and collapses model capabilities. Furthermore, the attack hushes up the watchdogs themselves: existing defenses often fail to detect the sabotage entirely or falsely attribute the amplified downstream anomalies to honest workers. Our findings expose a fundamental gap between local numerical verification and end-to-end accountability: perturbations too small to indict a worker locally can nevertheless be powerful enough to break distributed inference globally.

</details>

Abstract: The security guidance that bears on code is real but scattered. It lives across secure-development frameworks, verification standards, controls catalogues, threat taxonomies, and, increasingly, normative regimes such as the EU's NIS2 -- and most of it is written for readers other than the engineer, or the AI assistant, at a point of change. Existing cybersecurity ontologies largely model attacks, indicators, and risk for detection and analysis, not the engineering-practice substance one must act on. This paper presents a use case for an existing bounded application-security ontology, AppSec Core, whose construction and validation are reported separately. We show how it is used. It makes the engineering-actionable fraction of those authorities addressable as a typed working set: the control objectives that apply, the practices that realize them, the mechanisms that implement them, and the evidence artifacts they expect, each traceable to the sources that justify it. We reach that working set from two entry points that recur in practice. Forward, from a code change: a change to authentication middleware scopes to its governing domain slice and returns the obligations the change must satisfy. Inverse, from a normative obligation: "make this NIS2-compliant" resolves a generic legal duty into the concrete engineering obligations it implies across slices, surfacing precisely the fraction of a mostly-governance regulation that lands in code. The same working set is what a human engineer reads and what an LLM-grounded assistant is handed as grounding. The use turns "read every applicable authority" into "satisfy this typed working set", for a code change or a compliance duty alike. The contribution is the demonstrated use; the artefact, its validation, and its governance are companion work.

</details>

Abstract: We present the first large-scale privacy and accessibility evaluation of cookie notices across 2,061 domains for blind and low vision (BLV) users. Using a Playwright-based crawler, a custom NVDA screen reader add-on, and the Lighthouse and WAVE accessibility tools, we measure UK GDPR/PECR compliance, tracker activity across pre-interaction, post-accept, and post-reject phases, and cookie notice accessibility for screen reader users. We find that 51 % of sites display a cookie notice, of which (at least) 50 % violate UK GDPR/PECR requirements. Accepting a notice produces a mean five-fold increase in tracker activity, and degrades page landmark structure on one in five sites. While 95 % of notices are readable by a screen reader, only 37 % carry a meaningful title, 39 % are announced immediately on page load, and 42 % use descriptive button labels. We reveal a systemic correlation: cookie notices failing legal privacy requirements disproportionately exhibit the weakest screen reader accessibility. This compounding privacy burden on BLV users demands urgent intervention from developers, regulators, and standards bodies.

Abstract: Gaussian differential privacy (GDP) and zero-concentrated differential privacy (zCDP) are two competing privacy accounting frameworks, but they share similar minimax lower bounds in various estimation problems. Moreover, it is known that a mechanism satisfying $μ$-GDP satisfies $(μ^2/2)$-zCDP, whereas the converse fails to hold at the same calibration. In this paper, we establish the optimal reverse conversion, showing that every $ρ$-zCDP mechanism satisfies $μ_{\mathrm{zCDP}}(ρ)$-GDP, where $μ_{\mathrm{zCDP}}(ρ)=2Φ^{-1}((1+e^{-t_ρ})^{-1})$ and $t_ρ$ uniquely solves the equation $ρ=t_ρ\tanh(t_ρ/2)$. We also obtain simplified bounds $\sqrt{πρ}\leqμ_{\mathrm{zCDP}}(ρ)\leq\sqrt{8ρ}$, with sharp constants, which imply equivalence of the two frameworks up to constants. This explains why their minimax risks agree up to a constant rescaling of the privacy budget. We show that the squared conversion is strictly convex, which implies that converting zCDP to GDP before applying composition or group privacy yields a tighter GDP guarantee than converting afterward. We also derive the sharp conversion from bounded-range and logistic DP mechanisms (e.g., exponential mechanism) to GDP, where converting before composition but after group privacy gives the tighter guarantee.

</details>

Abstract: Sendrier's Support Splitting Algorithm (SSA) is the standard solver for the Permutation Equivalence Problem (PEP); its cost is dominated by a term that grows exponentially in the hull dimension. We study, mathematically, how much this cost can be lowered by replacing the standard inner product, on which the hull is defined, with an alternative bilinear form. Two structural results give a complete answer: only a narrow two-parameter family of bilinear forms produces a hull on which SSA can still operate, namely those whose matrix in the standard basis is a linear combination of the identity and the all-ones matrix; and even within this family the hull dimension drops by at most one (with the reduction achieved on every self-dual code whose length is coprime to the field characteristic). Together, these results give a tight upper bound: since SSA on a code with hull dimension $h$ has cost dominated by $q^h$, replacing the standard hull with $\mathrm{Hull}_M$ lowers this dominant term from $q^h$ to at best $q^{h-1}$, so no bilinear-form generalization of the inner product can reduce the SSA cost by more than a factor of $q$, the field size. Going beyond this requires invariants outside the bilinear framework. As an application, we verify that PEP-based SPECK admits the factor-$q$ cost reduction, while LEP-based LESS and PKP-based PERK lie outside our framework. A SageMath implementation of the bilinear-hull preprocessing confirms feasibility at SPECK's recommended length $n = 252$.

</details>

Abstract: Prefill jailbreaks can cause safety-aligned language models to continue an unsafe assistant prefix even when they would refuse the same request under normal decoding. We study how the model's internal harmfulness and refusal signals evolve along this attacker-controlled trajectory. Using the Jacobian lens, we track vocabulary-grounded harmfulness and refusal signals from the assistant header through prefilling and subsequent generation. We identify a characteristic phenomenon, which we call refusal-state erosion: both the refusal signal and the harmfulness signal weaken as the prefill progresses, but the refusal signal progressively declines to the benign baseline by the end of the prefill, while the harmfulness signal remains above its benign baseline and detectable during subsequent generation. Motivated by this observation, we introduce J-Anchor, a training-free inference-time defense that uses the model's pre-attack harmfulness and refusal signals as a request-specific anchor and selectively restores weakened refusal in J-space during prefilling and decoding. J-Anchor substantially reduces attack success under both fixed and deep prefill attacks across different model families. J-Anchor largely preserves benign behavior and reasoning ability, and ablations highlight the importance of targeted restoration in the J-space refusal subspace. Our results suggest that preserving latent refusal dynamics offers a promising route to robust defense against prefill jailbreaks.

</details>

Abstract: Cryptographically secure inference faces two practical challenges: making secure programming accessible to AI developers and data scientists, and achieving efficient execution across deployment environments. Developers must compose cryptographic primitives correctly, while performance depends on hardware, networks, and evolving ciphertext states. We present CipherFlow, a compiler framework that takes a high-level AI computation graph, available cryptographic primitives, and a target hardware and network configuration to generate an executable, latency-optimized secure inference program. CipherFlow automates cryptographic execution decisions through three techniques: MicroBench profiles deployment-specific primitive costs; state-expanded planning jointly optimizes homomorphic encryption (HE) and secure multi-party computation (MPC) placement, conversions, and ciphertext maintenance under cryptographic-state constraints; and BlockFold summarizes branched regions to scale planning to Transformer graphs. Evaluation using measured HE/MPC backend costs across CPU/GPU and network settings shows up to 16.5$\times$ speedup on BERT-base and over 22$\times$ on ViT compared with static hybrid placement baselines. CipherFlow separates model programming from cryptographic execution decisions, enabling developers to express AI computations while the compiler constructs efficient secure programs.

</details>

Abstract: Advances in monocular depth estimation (MDE) now let an attacker generate depth from publicly available RGB images and submit forged RGB-D queries. Such attacks break a core assumption of RGB-D place authentication: that depth comes from a sensor. We study a presentation attack detection (PAD) classifier that uses geometric consistency between the enrollment and query views, as a software-layer defense. A three-stage evaluation shows how far this classifier detects software-injected depth, depending on how accurately the attacker knows the camera intrinsics. In the first stage, the attacker has only an estimate of the intrinsics, and the PAD classifier separates forgeries generated by all three MDE models with a validation area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.997-0.999. In the second stage, the attacker obtains the exact intrinsics, and the original PAD classifier falls to near chance (AUC 0.54-0.61); a retrained PAD classifier, however, still has the registration residual between enrollment and query as a cue. In the third stage, an oracle evaluation, in which the evaluator fits warps using the ground-truth 3D geometry, shows that detection falls to near chance only in its strongest case, where every valid query keypoint is given its ground-truth 3D coordinate. Of the detection cues, the reprojection error captures the attacker's misestimate of the intrinsics, whereas the registration residual remains even when the attacker knows the exact intrinsics.

</details>

Abstract: Currently, long-chain LLM agents commonly suffer from behavioral degradation in complex tasks, where prompts are written in full but steps are not executed in full. To address this issue, existing approaches face three major challenges. First, constraints are strongly bound to their textual carriers; once natural-language constraint sentences are removed, their binding force vanishes. Second, the execution process lacks independent verification, as the evidence for judgment is often supplied by the executing party itself, leading to the failure of self-referential validation. Third, existing remediation schemes are difficult to generalize, and hard-coded mechanisms cannot be transferred across tasks. In this paper, we propose StateGate, a novel prompt-optimization scheme that transforms semantic constraints into symbolic gates enforced by an external deterministic runtime. By decoupling the content of a constraint from its carrier of efficacy, StateGate achieves behavioral fidelity in long-chain tasks without losing the binding force of the prompt. Specifically, we first propose four independent compression strategies (deduplication, generalization, externalization, and text-to-gating) together with their corresponding decision chains, enabling principled prompt symbolization. Second, we design a three-component architecture consisting of a state file, a validator, and an orchestrator, which thoroughly separates judgment authority from execution authority. Third, we formally prove that the compliance of symbolic constraints is independent of context size. Finally, we validate the framework on a vulnerability-discovery task in the security domain; the results show that StateGate achieves 100% execution closure and accurate vulnerability detection.

</details>

Abstract: The rapid growth of multiple blockchain ecosystems has made cross-chain bridges essential for transferring assets and information across independent blockchains. Their multi-component architectures, cross-chain trust assumptions, and concentration of valuable assets expose them to diverse security threats. This paper presents an empirical study of cross-chain bridge attacks based on 48 evidence-supported real-world incidents from January 2022 to August 2026. We propose a behaviour-based attack pattern architecture comprising six trust-violation attack patterns, each defined by the primary trust assumption violated by the attacker: Proof Forgery,Logic Exploitation, Event Injection, Economic-State Manipulation, Credential Seizure, and Infrastructure Hijacking. The architecture further maps these patterns to five functional domains of cross-chain bridges: Deposit, Relay, Verification, Execution, and Governance and Operations, separating how a trust assumption is violated from where the compromised trust boundary lies. The 48 incidents correspond to $2.676 billion in gross bridge-specific losses, with the ten largest incidents accounting for 92.4% of total losses. Measured on the primary loss basis, Proof Forgery and Credential Seizure make up 86.2% of all losses, and the Verification and Governance and Operations domains hold 93.9%. A handful of very large incidents drives this concentration, yet it still holds when we switch to other loss bases or reclassify the boundary cases. By tying what attackers do to the bridge functions and security assumptions they target, the architecture gives threat modelling for cross-chain bridges an empirical footing.

</details>

Abstract: Email spam and phishing remain persistent threats, and purely algorithmic classifiers may struggle with unfamiliar senders, evolving message content, and cold-start domains. This study evaluates whether Reddit-derived community signals can serve as a supplementary domain-reputation feature in email spam classification. We use a preprocessed SpamAssassin-derived corpus of 5,582 emails and compare three feature configurations: (1) a baseline using TF-IDF and structural features; (2) a traditional-enhanced configuration adding urgency and action scores; and (3) a Reddit-enhanced configuration adding a binary discussion-presence indicator, a keyword-based Reddit score, and an urgency-Reddit interaction term. We train Logistic Regression, Random Forest, and Support Vector Machine models. In the preliminary single-split evaluation, the Random Forest model with Reddit-enhanced features achieves 97.94% accuracy, a 0.55 percentage-point improvement over the baseline, although the improvement is not statistically significant in the primary test split. Logistic Regression performance declines when additional features are introduced, and the SVM performs poorly and is treated as a negative control. Reddit signals appear most informative for rare domains, but approximately 70% of emails receive no Reddit-derived signal. Because the Reddit data are temporally later than the email corpus, we do not interpret the results as evidence of real-time detection. Instead, the study provides a conservative evaluation of community-derived signals as a supplementary, asynchronous domain-reputation feature and identifies the additional validation required before production deployment, including domain-grouped validation, temporally aligned contemporary data, and adversarial robustness testing.

Abstract: Coding-agent services are priced by the model they claim, making the verification of such model claims crucial for their users. In this paper, we reveal that existing model auditing methods rely on a latent assumption that the deployment is static, i.e., the claimed model is always served in one reference configuration. This assumption does not hold for coding-agent services, whose providers may legitimately change the sampling preset, the reasoning mode or the system prompt without changing the model. As a result, existing methods either falsely accuse honest providers or miss cheap derivatives of the claimed model. To address this problem, we propose a certified model-claim verification method (dubbed CertClaim). Instead of testing the service against one reference deployment, we make the authorized deployments part of the claim and test whether some authorized deployment explains all the evidence of the service, by calibrating multiple evidence channels in every authorized deployment and fusing them within each deployment. We provide theoretical analyses of its false-accusation rate and detection condition. Extensive experiments on eight claims, two agent harnesses and three task suites verify the effectiveness of CertClaim, which is valid in every setting while separating substitutes far better than existing methods, as well as its resistance to potential adaptive attacks.

</details>

Abstract: Existing attacks and defenses for coding agents act once content enters the model context: injected text persuades the model, and guards inspect what the model reads. Yet to judge whether processing an unknown archive, network response, or image is safe, an agent often must process it first, and that step can do harm before any content reaches the model. We call this first processing Step Zero, and harm done by it a Step-Zero attack. Across seven harnesses and six models, each agent received only a path or URL to a crafted input, paired with a benign twin showing the same visible fields. Step Zero is where most agents are already unsafe: 32/42 harness-model pairs overran a 4 MiB disk budget, 26/42 before the model saw any member, and four of the six models did so in every round (84/84), so the damage precedes any model-side defense. Every twin stayed within budget, so the attacker's bytes alone cause the harm. On network and image inputs the risk moves to what the agent writes: agents stored the body under the name the input declared, letting the input decide what is written, and their checks faded once cues were removed. Model-free tests trace the harm to the processing primitive, so we defend it there: a gate that counts the bytes each call produces held every routed extraction within budget while agents completed 25/28 tasks. Vendors should pair such gates with safety-alignment training that teaches agents to inspect an input before processing it.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model based agents increasingly rely on external tools to invoke services and interact with web resources. These tools can evolve through software updates, yet agentic frameworks may continue trusting them without re-verifying their safety after implementation changes. This tool update gap creates a supply-chain vulnerability in agentic systems: because updated tool code executes outside the LLM's context window, malicious functionality embedded in tool updates can bypass defenses centered on model-visible interactions, leaving agents exposed to stealthy, persistent threats that existing in-context defenses can hardly address.
  To thoroughly investigate this threat, we introduce ETUDE (Exploiting Tool Updates for Direct Execution), an attack that embeds malicious functionality into updates of initially benign tools to produce compromised tools, while preserving their legitimate tool functionality. Unlike prompt-based attacks, ETUDE operates entirely outside the LLM's context and requires no adversarial instructions, thereby bypassing defenses restricted to model-visible content. We evaluate ETUDE across three representative attack workloads and seven widely used commercial and open-source agentic frameworks, including Gemini CLI, Codex CLI, and Claude Code. The results show that ETUDE achieves a 97.6% ASR while maintaining a 95.9% legitimate task completion rate.
  We further examine existing defenses and find that they provide insufficient protection against this threat. These findings expose a fundamental blind spot in current agent security practices: trust established for a tool does not necessarily remain valid after its implementation updates.

</details>

Abstract: WebAssembly (Wasm) runs untrusted code in a portable, lightweight sandbox and is increasingly used to host services on shared servers and edge platforms. Running the QUIC transport protocol inside Wasm would let each application choose and update its own transport without changing the host. QUIC, however, encrypts every packet, and Wasm offers neither dedicated AES instructions nor carry-less multiplication; a Wasm QUIC stack has been reported to reach only 17.6% of native goodput, with cryptography as its largest cost. Whether the 128-bit SIMD extension of Wasm (WASM-SIMD) can recover this cost, and what limits a pure-Wasm stack once it does, has remained unclear. Here we show that explicit WASM-SIMD kernels recover much of the cryptographic cost, after which non-cryptographic costs become the limit. On two hosts connected by 10-Gigabit Ethernet, kernels that process eight AES blocks or four ChaCha20 blocks per pass raise receiver goodput over a non-SIMD baseline by 61.34% for AES-128-GCM and 31.55% for ChaCha20-Poly1305, whereas compiler auto-vectorization gains only 1.28% for AES-128-GCM. ChaCha20-Poly1305 still delivers 1.99 times the goodput of AES-128-GCM, confirming end to end the preference that stand-alone cipher benchmarks suggest. Receiver-side profiling combined with Amdahl's law predicts that even native-speed cryptography would leave Wasm at roughly one third of the goodput of the fastest native stack. Cryptographic acceleration is therefore necessary but not sufficient: memory allocation, runtime overhead and per-datagram UDP I/O now bound pure-Wasm transports. We anticipate that this per-category cost breakdown will guide further optimization of Wasm network stacks and the design of system interfaces for batched I/O.

</details>

Abstract: Many privacy regulations, including the California Consumer Privacy Act (CCPA), require websites to honor users' opt-out choices. To facilitate this process, industry has introduced technical standards such as the IAB Global Privacy Platform (GPP), which aims to standardize collection and transmission of user consent information across multiple jurisdictions. However, it remains unclear whether these standards consistently and correctly transmit user opt-out choices, or whether opt-out choices are respected by advertisers.
  In this study, we conduct an in-depth analysis of consent propagation under the GPP framework. We collect data from four web crawls across four experimental settings to analyze how widely the standard is adopted, the extent to which opt-out signals propagate from publishers to third parties, and whether these signals reach and alter third-party advertiser behavior. Our analysis reveals several inconsistencies in the implementation and adoption of the standard that result in user consent signals being lost and user opt-out choices not being respected by the advertisers.

The resulting framework is called BELL (Budgeted E-evidence Localization for LLM Watermarks). Theoretically, BELL can control the token-weighted false discovery rate (FDR) without independence assumptions across regions. Finally, extensive experimental results demonstrate that BELL consistently outperforms competing methods across multiple LLMs, datasets, and watermarking schemes.

Abstract: Quantized neural networks store weights in dense memory, where bit flips caused by voltage scaling, aging, radiation, or deliberate attacks such as RowHammer can severely degrade model behavior. A single corrupted high-order bit can cause misclassifications or nonsensical language-model outputs. Embedded error-correcting codes (ECC) avoid dedicated parity storage by hiding parity within model weights, but existing schemes embed uniformly across layers and bit planes, leave parity unsecured, and tolerate bit-error rates (BERs) only up to about 6%. An adversary aware of sensitive weights and the ECC scheme can also bypass protection by exceeding the correction capability of targeted codewords. We propose SANAI, a sensitivity-guided, bit-plane-selective scheme that protects critical model weights against bit flips with zero additional parity-storage overhead. SANAI secures parity through keyed embedding across a two-dimensional weight space: spread-transform dither modulation binds parity to a secret key, and keyed interleaving disperses concentrated attacks across codewords. We derive a survival law showing that robustness is governed by the correctable fraction t/n rather than by t alone, and a closed-form bound on the protectable fraction of a model. As a streaming checker on an 8x8 systolic array, SANAI verifies weights on the load path with 0.41% area overhead and no additional memory. Across ten vision backbones, three quantization schemes, and ten language models from 82M to 13B parameters, SANAI stays within about one percentage point of clean accuracy at BERs up to 12%, twice the maximum of prior embedded-ECC approaches. It survives fine-tuning bit-exactly, neutralizes progressive and targeted bit-flip attacks, and exactly corrects AttentionBreaker and SilentStriker attacks.

</details>

Abstract: The increasing penetration of Distributed Energy Resources (DERs) in the smart grid has significantly expanded the cyber-attack surface. Traditionally, DER networks rely on static role-based access control (RBAC) mechanisms that assume persistent trust after authentication. This limitation enables adversaries to maintain unauthorized privileges and move laterally across DER systems, disrupting grid operations. Although attribute-based access control (ABAC) enables fine-grained and context-aware access decisions, it does not fully eliminate implicit trust. Zero Trust (ZT) principles can be incorporated to enhance ABAC policies. This paper proposes a dynamic access control framework for implementing ZT in DER networks (ZT-DAC). To support realistic DER operations, a DER-specific access control model is developed and used to generate a domain-informed dataset of access requests incorporating both legitimate and adversarial behaviors. Experimental results on the dataset show that the OPA-based decision layer achieves \(91.92\%\) accuracy, with a False Positive Rate of \(14.45\%\), while the ML model achieves a Mean Absolute Error of \(2.074\) and an \(R^2\) of \(0.891\) in trust-score prediction. The ABAC layer achieves \(93.27\%\) accuracy, with a FPR of \(3.88\%\), validating its practical feasibility within realistic DER environments.

Abstract: Machine learning models have shown exceptional accuracy for ballot classification in election systems. Recent research has demonstrated that these ballot classification models are vulnerable to adversarial example attacks. This is a critical security issue because a successful attack could alter the outcome of an election and undermine the democratic process. However, all adversarial example attacks in the voting domain have assumed a white-box threat model, where the classifier parameters are known. In this work, we advance the field of machine learning in election systems by analyzing a realistic black-box adversary. We implement and evaluate eleven state-of-the-art black-box attacks: query based attacks ADBA, Square, SurFree, RayS, and SA-MOO. We also test adaptive transfer based black-box attacks with APGD-$l_\infty$, APGD-$l_2$, APGD-$l_1$, PGD $l_0$, PGD $l_0$+$l_\infty$, and PGD $l_0$+$σ$. Our attacks span multiple different norms ($l_\infty$, $l_2$, $l_1$, and $l_0$) and three different black-box adversarial threat models (decision based, score based and transfer based). In addition to digital analyses, we physically print and scan $20,000$ adversarial examples to evaluate the real-world performance of different black-box attacks. Our finding demonstrate an even larger physical-digital robustness gap exists for black-box adversarial examples than for white-box adversarial examples. Lastly, we conduct the first ever adversarial example transferability study in the voting domain and test adversarial training and barrier zones as two possible black-box defense strategies.

For designers, the findings caution against treating publisher count as independent evidence, notification as repair, or cleared histories as recovery. Abstract: Indirect prompt injection creates a distinctive risk for blue-team Large Language Model (LLM) agents in Security Operations Centers (SOCs). Malicious instructions can be embedded in telemetry, tickets, retrieval results, or memory and then propagate through alert triage, incident summarization, response recommendation, and Indicator of Compromise (IOC) extraction as locally plausible intermediate decisions. We characterize this failure mode as evidence-chain contamination of protected task state. The main challenge is task dependent: a suspicious string may be malicious control text for summarization but essential evidence for IOC extraction. To address this challenge, we propose StateFence, a task-aware evaluation and defense framework that treats severity labels, incident narratives, response policies, and IOC sets as protected states. StateFence maps indirect prompt-injection attacks into explicit, stealth, multi-hop, and persistent SOC evidence attacks, records observable propagation traces, and routes defenses according to the attacked state. Across controlled SOC cases and public-SOC validation cases, StateFence lowers attack success and reduces failures that appear only after evidence is poisoned, while preserving task utility and avoiding the indicator loss caused by task-mismatched filtering. Our findings indicate that robust blue-team LLM agents require mechanisms that preserve task-critical state under untrusted evidence.

Abstract: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) has become a leading standard for connecting Large Language Models (LLMs) to external tools, software, and data sources. MCP architecture favors ease of integration over security, blending the data and control planes into a single natural language channel. This introduces confused deputy and prompt injection vulnerabilities that cannot be completely mitigated in the software development lifecycle (SDLC) of an MCP server. Structured security guidance for server developers has been outpaced by this adoption trend, leaving them without a clear way to identify where vulnerabilities can be addressed at the server layer and where architectural constraints create hard limits. This paper presents a framework based on OWASP MCP Top 10 mapped to three architectural tiers, developed using Design Science Research (DSR). Vulnerabilities are classified as server-primary, gateway-primary, or protocol-primary applying a cascading decision tree following four questions, resulting in reproducible tier assignments and confidence levels independent of who applies it. The framework was validated against an enterprise-grade Managed File Transfer (MFT) MCP server built in Go. Validation results showed that high-confidence server-tier controls worked as predicted, and low-confidence protocol-tier controls failed for architectural reasons beyond server implementation. The architectural ceiling encountered during practical validation is the main contribution and shows how secure-by-design development can reduce the MCP attack surface but not completely mitigate it.

Abstract: Companies deploy large language model (LLM) assistants on their websites to advise customers. For commercial reasons, the operator can give an assistant a goal that the customer never sees. This paper focuses on operator-induced vulnerability-targeted manipulation (VTM), in which an assistant pursues such a goal by exploiting a vulnerability the customer discloses. While existing research mainly measures how manipulative a model is, we go further and ask whether VTM can be detected from the customer-AI conversation. To investigate this at scale, we introduce GAP (Goal-Anchored Probing). GAP first records the option a model recommends without any goal and then sets the other option as the operator's goal. We apply GAP to 15 open- and closed-source LLMs and collect 619,600 labeled replies across 540 everyday scenarios in 12 domains. We organize our study around three research questions. (1) Do assistants with an operator goal target vulnerable customers? (2) Can VTM be detected from the customer-AI conversation? (3) Does detection generalize to unseen models, vulnerabilities, and instruction styles? We first find that 14 of 15 models push the operator goal, and 12 of them use disclosed distress or financial strain to support it. Next, we show that fine-tuned detectors identify exploitative pushes with 79\% accuracy from the reply alone, while zero-shot LLM judges average at most 62\%. Finally, detection weakens on vulnerabilities absent from training. Telling whether a push comes from the operator also becomes unreliable when the assistant is told to hide its goal. We will release our dataset and code to facilitate future research on auditing LLM assistants.

Abstract: As large language model (LLM) agents take on more complex tasks with greater autonomy, security has become a major obstacle to their broader deployment. Unlike in attacks that require complex exploitation of operating system vulnerabilities, attackers can easily hijack agents through indirect prompt injection (IPI), leading to privacy leakage, financial loss, and destructive operations. However, existing defenses often make security decisions based on limited static trusted evidence or assess observations in isolation. Such rigid trust assumptions can cause task-essential information to be unnecessarily discarded, resulting in over-defense and degraded agent functionality. To address this problem, we propose ATrust, a defense mechanism that adaptively expands the trust boundary during agent runtime. Starting from the user query as the root trust anchor, ATrust evaluates external observations against the current trust boundary and execution state, progressively admitting task-essential information as new trust anchors. This preserves necessary environmental feedback while excluding irrelevant content, whether benign or malicious, from subsequent decision-making, thereby limiting unnecessary exposure to external environment. We evaluate ATrust on AgentDojo and AgentDyn, where it achieves the strongest overall security--utility trade-off among existing defenses. With GPT-4o as the engine model, ATrust reduces the attack success rate on AgentDyn from 37.80% to 4.37% while enhancing utility by 10.00%, outperforming AuthGraph, the state-of-the-art defense, by 12.33%. These results demonstrate the effectiveness and practicality of ATrust.

Abstract: Encrypted semantic communication systems protect transmitted messages by inserting key-conditioned modules into a deep-learning semantic communication pipeline, but existing designs rely on a fixed adversarial weight and operate mainly at the semantic-feature level. We propose two changes to the encrypted semantic communication system (ESCS): a key-conditioned signal-domain perturbation layer, called learned adversarial perturbation injection (LAPI), and an information noise-contrastive estimation (InfoNCE)-guided adaptive training rule that {adds an adaptive leakage penalty alongside the fixed adversarial objective} using an InfoNCE-based proxy for mutual information leakage. Experiments on the Europarl corpus over additive white Gaussian noise (AWGN) and Rayleigh fading channels show that InfoNCE-guided training is the primary source of eavesdropper suppression, reducing the eavesdropper's bilingual evaluation understudy (BLEU) score to approximately 0.07 at high signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). Adding LAPI to the InfoNCE-guided objective further lowers the InfoNCE-estimated leakage by 5-9% with low measured inference overhead, while its impact on legitimate-receiver BLEU depends on channel conditions. The two mechanisms operate on different parts of the pipeline and can be jointly applied.

Abstract: Single-pass scoring models such as Jev give each code function a vulnerability probability, so that security teams can rank many functions for review without generating written explanations. Training an open-weight model for this task is limited by data. Benchmarks with both vulnerable and non-vulnerable labels keep few vulnerable functions once examples related to the test data are removed, while larger public vulnerability records mostly contain vulnerable functions only. Mixing the two lets the data source predict the label, and training on the labeled data alone leaves a model that often scores a fixed function above its vulnerable original. We present Cyber-JEV, a fine-tuning method that uses fix commits to turn these records into comparison groups. Each group holds a vulnerable function, its patched version and up to two unchanged functions from the same file, so the model sees the change that removed a recorded defect. One shared model learns two tasks: classifying functions from the labeled reference data, and ranking the vulnerable function above the other members of its group. At test time the input is one function and the output is a vulnerability score. On a test benchmark of 7,270 functions, an ensemble of five fine-tuned Qwen3.5-4B models reaches an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 0.814, compared with 0.743 for Jev 1.13. With the 4,031 alerts of Jev as the review budget, it misses 16 of 322 vulnerable functions instead of 39. Compared with training on the reference data alone, it ranks the vulnerable version above the patched version 14.8 to 29.2 percentage points more often on three sets of real fixes. Such scores can order the changed functions of a pull request or a release for review under a fixed budget.

Abstract: Leakage attacks on secret sharing schemes can be based on the exploitation of partial information about individual shares to recover the underlying secret. In coding theory, linear exact repair schemes (LERSs) allow the recovery of a codeword symbol from limited information obtained from the remaining symbols. When applied to code-based secret sharing schemes, such repair schemes can be interpreted as recovering the secret from partial information about the shares, expressed in terms of subfield symbols. Guruswami and Wootters introduced the first explicit constructions of LERSs for low-rate Reed--Solomon codes that is optimal in terms of bandwidth. In this work, we first investigate the construction and algebraic properties of LERSs, building on the Guruswami-Wootters construction. We derive new explicit repair schemes from their construction and establish structural properties of the resulting schemes. Second, we further develop a systematic computational approach based on a Gr{ö}bner bases approach to identify leakage functions associated with LERSs for arbitrary linear codes. This approach enables us to investigate the existence and structural properties of LERSs across different code families and parameter ranges. We first applied our method to Reed-Solomon codes, which gave as further insights into the algebraic structure of LERSs. Moreover, by applying our method to generalized Reed-Muller and Tamo-Barg codes, we identify several LERSs that, to the best of our knowledge, have not previously been described in the literature.

</details>

Abstract: In sequential data analysis, privacy loss accumulates over time, and privacy filters and odometers are used to control and track this cumulative cost. We study how the information used by differential privacy filters and odometers fundamentally limits what they can achieve. We formalise three levels of information: spec-level information, fixed prior to the interaction; mechanism-level information, which includes the mechanisms instantiated during the interaction; and realisation-level information, which further includes the realised interaction. We classify privacy filters and odometers by the least informative level sufficient to determine their behaviour.
  Our main results establish strict and arbitrarily large separations in achievable performance across this information hierarchy. For privacy filters, moving up the hierarchy can yield an unbounded utility advantage, quantified by a clipped measure of the number of allowed releases. For privacy odometers, it can yield an arbitrarily large improvement in precision, quantified by the expected peak excess of the reported bound over the realised privacy loss. Further, although the information classes are distinct, we show that moving to a richer class need incur essentially no loss in performance.
  Finally, we show that randomisation provides a resource orthogonal to information: at every information level, it can yield an arbitrarily large utility improvement over deterministic filters. Thus, information access and randomisation can be viewed as fundamental resources governing privacy procedures in adaptive interactions.

</details>

Abstract: Deploying AI at the edge increases the need for security features on existing devices. When physical access to the device is available, storing secret keys in non-volatile memory is vulnerable. Physical unclonable functions (PUFs) address this by exploiting the unique yet repeatable characteristics of each device, but conventional implementations require design-stage modifications, making them inapplicable to a vast number of existing devices already manufactured. Recently, software PUFs have emerged as an alternative approach, leveraging analog characteristics exposed through software-only interfaces to derive unique responses, without post-fabrication changes. Since edge AI hardware is bound to long operational lifespans, this offers clear economic and engineering value. Existing research on software PUFs remains fragmented. It lacks a holistic review of the trade-offs among choices of components used as entropy sources and software methods to expose analog characteristics. This gap hinders assessing feasibility, comparing approaches, and identifying open challenges towards a hardware root of trust in existing devices. This paper surveys 37 existing software PUF works and compares them against the requirements of on-device model protection: boot-time versus runtime key derivation, and response latency. It analyzes trade-offs, outlines four key challenges that current software PUFs face for edge AI deployment, and highlights promising directions for overcoming barriers to real-world adoption.

</details>

Abstract: LLM agents compact long histories to fit their context windows, but the compacted context must still carry the operating rules that govern later actions. Prior work shows that violations rise after compaction, but measures compliance outside a complete agent harness and does not identify where each violation comes from. We introduce ARCBench(Agent Rule Compliance Benchmark), a configurable benchmark built on the open-source pi agent harness. Its tool-calling agent loop lets us measure rule compliance from actions taken during execution, bringing the evaluation closer to deployed agent workflows. Using ARCBench, we examine where violations after compaction come from, and how different compaction configurations and adversarial settings change their sources. We find that compaction rarely deletes the rule: most violations after compaction occur while the rule is still in the compacted history, with the agent treating a later request as permission, ignoring the rule, and proceeding anyway. Where violations come from depends on the configuration: how the rule is delivered decides whether the agent would break it even without compaction, while the compaction strategy and budget decide whether the rule survives. The strongest compactor-targeted attack more than doubles violations by corrupting the rule in the summary and changing how the agents behave, whereas the strongest defense restores the rule in most summary but still violates. Finally, retaining a rule does not guarantee compliance: compacted runs that retain the rule violate more than twice as often as runs with the full history.

</details>

Abstract: Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) enables privacy-preserving computation on encrypted data without decryption. Despite its strong security guarantees, FHE incurs substantial computational and memory overhead. In FHE-based deep learning inference, on average 63% of the CPU time is spent on the number theoretic transform (NTT) and its inverse (INTT), which reduce the complexity of polynomial multiplication from $O(N^2)$ to $O(N \log N)$. Prior FPGA works follow two approaches: accelerating entire FHE schemes, which yields significant speedups but ties them to specific parameters and algorithms, or accelerating only the NTT, which applies across schemes, algorithms, and libraries. Most FPGA NTT designs require interconnects that grow with parallelism and utilize significant resources, which complicates routing on multi-die FPGAs. Additionally, to the best of our knowledge, end-to-end NTT acceleration within an FHE context has so far been left unexplored. We design a fully unrolled, delay-commutator streaming NTT/INTT kernel, and floorplan it across the three dies of an AMD Alveo V80. At 400 MHz, the kernel transforms a polynomial of $2^{16}$ coefficients every 5.12 $μ$s, the highest throughput among FPGA designs at this size. Offloaded end to end, it is up to 22x faster than the CPU per transform. We integrate the kernel into the Lattigo FHE library and evaluate it end to end. With 128-bit secure parameters, it accelerates core FHE operations, such as key switching and bootstrapping, by 1.8-2.2x, and neural network inference by 1.8-2.1x.

</details>

Abstract: Privacy-preserving approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) search allows clients to search from an untrusted server without revealing sensitive queries, and is useful in scenarios like medical diagnostics and credit scoring. Among the various privacy-preserving primitives, trusted execution environments (TEEs) have much lower overhead than heavy cryptographic ones, yet they leak memory access patterns, enabling adversaries to infer query contents.
  Oblivious RAM (ORAM) can be used to hide those access patterns at $\mathrm{O}(\log N)$ asymptotic cost, but existing high-performance ANN search algorithms (e.g., IVF or HNSW) exhibit complex, data-dependent access patterns that are unfriendly for ORAM. Furthermore, standard ORAM constructions typically assume there exists a small amount of trusted storage, which weakens the security guarantees and is not always feasible. Solutions that eliminate such trusted storage (known as {\em double obliviousness}) typically degrade performance further to $\mathrm{O}(\log^2 N)$.
  We introduce O2N2, a doubly oblivious ANN framework built around single-path search in randomly rotated $k$-d tree forests. A pointer-based Circuit ORAM stores the search structure, with separate storage for compact internal nodes and vector-bearing leaves. Each visited leaf is processed by an oblivious selection network, and fixed-schedule duplicate removal and selection combine the local results into the final top-$k$.
  We evaluate O2N2 on real-world datasets and further test its scalability on 100 million vectors. For 10-NN and 100-NN search at the specified high-recall targets, it achieves a $9.8$--$125.9\times$ speedup over the fastest doubly oblivious baselines.

</details>
