Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can synthesize Amazon Web Services (AWS) Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies from tasks and operational documents. Attacker-controlled context may induce provider-valid policies that exceed trusted authorization requirements. We present IAMRAGBENCH, a 60-case benchmark spanning scope, conditions, explicit denies, trust, permissions boundaries, and role delegation. It combines deterministic authorization-envelope scoring with case-specific validation, including request-level checks where supported. An attack requires a provider-valid clean policy that satisfies the task and a provider-valid treatment policy that violates the envelope, matches a predeclared goal, preserves an intended request, and newly authorizes a forbidden request.
  Across five endpoints, included-context exposure produces 49 exact authorization deltas, including 46 goal-matched attacks (46/292; 15.75\%). End-to-end BM25 over 720 documents retrieves the designated poison for all 60 tasks at \(k=5\) but produces 7/294 goal-matched attacks (2.38\%). Presenting the same weakening as a final security architecture exception produces 74/116 attacks in a frozen two-endpoint stress test (63.79\%) and 115/294 in a post hoc all-five extension using the same frozen prompts (39.12\%). In a replay of 194 provider-valid treatment policies that violate task requirements, AWS \texttt{CheckNoNewAccess} reports no new access for four of 168 goal-matched exact attacks. The full pre-deployment rule rejects all 171 exact deltas and routes five unresolved cases to review. These findings support treating IAM copilots as untrusted proposal generators whose outputs require independent authorization checks before deployment.

</details>

Abstract: We compare the signing and verification performance of three Verifiable Credential (VC) formats--SD-JWT VC; the W3C Verifiable Credentials Data Model 2.0 with a Data Integrity cryptosuite (W3C VCDM); and mdoc (ISO/IEC 18013-5)--over one scope: issue, present with selective disclosure, verify. Node.js, Go and Python implementations ran in four environments, one a cloud VM (N=2,000 across five runs; medians of each statistic and the spread). For an end-to-end exchange disclosing 5 of 20 attributes, the total (issue, present, verify) on Node.js in the cloud VM is 0.265 ms for mdoc, 0.298 ms for SD-JWT VC and 0.534 ms for W3C VCDM. The four span a factor of five in speed; across them W3C VCDM takes 1.73--2.04x mdoc and 1.57--1.79x SD-JWT VC--at least five times our 10% band--and the ordering survives unifying the algorithm on Ed25519 and P-256. The mdoc--SD-JWT VC difference is not robust: the totals differ by 12%, 28%, 19% and 10% in the four environments, and at verification mdoc is the slower in one of them. RDFC-1.0 canonicalization produces the gap: the cryptosuite canonicalizes twice per operation, so one exchange canonicalizes six times. We attribute that cost to the specification rather than to a library mainly by replacing RDFC-1.0 with JCS, which brings W3C VCDM verification to within 5% of SD-JWT VC in three environments and within 12% in the fourth; three implementations in three languages agree, though they share a lineage. Because eddsa-rdfc-2022 has no derivation step, presenting a subset re-signs it; ecdsa-sd-2023, which preserves the issuer signature, costs 3.4 ms to issue and 1.5--2.4 ms to derive and verify there. With production education schemas, canonicalization costs 31--49x a simple credential across the four, and blank node labeling is not the driver. The measured path ends at the issuer signature check.

### [335] [Attacking P2P Networks: Liveness Attacks on the GossipSub and DOG Gossip Protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03509)

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Peer-to-peer (P2P) networks form the communication layer of large decentralized systems such as Ethereum, and the correctness of higher-level protocols depends on the gossip layer delivering messages to honest nodes. We study the robustness of two gossip protocols against adversaries whose only goal is to violate liveness, that is, to prevent honest nodes from receiving fresh messages, without forging, altering, or invalidating any message.
  For GossipSub, the publish-subscribe protocol used by Ethereum, we show that an adversary operating a constant number of geographically distributed nodes with a fixed latency advantage can take over the ``First Message Deliveries'' peer-scoring term and separate a constant fraction of nodes from the honest mesh. We analyze the attack under a geographical model, address the random-peer and outbound-connection mechanisms, and show that the separation can be maintained under realistic churn.
  For DOG, a transaction-dissemination protocol designed for CometBFT, we first give simple counterexamples in which route filtering disconnects the dissemination graph even without failures or Byzantine participants, and then present a practical attack that uses only a single physical node with a constant number of identities and legitimate actions to disable every forwarding route, separating the full network while allowing only direct publisher-to-neighbor delivery. We verify the attack in a simulated network and analyze its cost, assumptions, and possible mitigations.

</details>

Abstract: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) lets agentic AI frameworks connect language models to external tool providers, but it carries attacker-controlled content (tool descriptions, schema fields, and response data) into model context without validation. Prior benchmarks measure model compliance with poisoned instructions without confirming real execution. We present the first cross-framework measurement of MCP tool poisoning with real tool execution. A 25-class root-cause taxonomy unifies prior classifications, and the open-source tool mcpvenom drives a 9,720-cell susceptibility matrix across 12 frameworks, 18 models, and 45 attack scenarios, with a dual oracle that confirms exfiltration via canary HTTP receipt. The overall attack success rate is 22.9% (2,113 vulnerable cells). Of these, 64.9% dispatch an attacker tool call, 23.0% contact the attacker endpoint, and 12.1% verifiably exfiltrate a secret. Resistance is model-level: nine core frameworks cluster within 23 to 26% (chi-squared = 3.30, p = 0.91), and no framework implements intentional defense. Incidental framework properties mitigate in specific cases, and a regex description scanner rejects 20 of 45 scenarios at a 7.2% false-positive rate, so metadata scanning alone is a partial control. Model ASR ranges from 0.4% to 49.7%. Claude Opus 4.8 is vulnerable in 8 of 530 cells while issuing 223 explicit refusals. A code-level audit finds no intentional defense across twelve frameworks, and all five coordinated disclosures across four vendors were rejected.

</details>
